Let me now carefully design the logical relation for CBV SN.

I'll follow a known approach (e.g., "Reducibility candidates for call-by-value" — the "CR" definition uses values). Let me define:

```
Definition sn (t : tm) : Prop := strongly_normalizing t.

We need the fundamental theorem over contexts with fvar substitution. Let me think about what's the easiest formalization given the locally nameless setup.

Approach: define the logical relation as a predicate `R T t` on (possibly open) terms, where we prove the fundamental theorem with an environment sigma : atom -> tm mapping free atoms to terms, and `R` closed under... but the T_Abs case requires the semantic interpretation of open bodies. Let's carefully work it out.

Define:
```
Inductive lrel : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| lr_bool : forall t, sn t -> lrel Ty_Bool t
| lr_arrow : forall T1 T2 t, sn t -> (forall v, value v -> lrel T1 v -> lrel T2 (tm_app t v)) -> lrel (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t.
```
(Using an Inductive or Fixpoint definition by type.)

Hmm, but do we need `sn (open b v)` for beta? For R(arrow) we require `lrel T2 (app t v)` for values v ∈ R T1. When t = abs T1 b, `app (abs T1 b) v --> open b v` and is a reduct. So `lrel T2 (app (abs T1 b) v)` would need `sn (app (abs T1 b) v)` — which requires all reducts SN including open b v, and if T2 is an arrow, for all w ∈ R T3, lrel T4 (app (app (abs...)v) w) ... **Alternative cleaner method**: use a different logical relation definition where arrow type is interpreted via "application to SN arguments gives SN" in a way that's closed under expansion by construction.

Actually, there's a classical trick for CBV SN (I recall from a paper "A simple proof of normalization for call-by-value" or from Loader's "Normalisation by evaluation"): define the relation on terms by

R_Bool t := SN t
R_{A→B} t := SN t ∧ ∀ v. V_A v → SN (t v)? 

where V_A := { v value | v ∈ R_A }? And then the fundamental theorem gives SN. Then the App case: t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A, need SN (t1 t2). We need a lemma: if t1 ∈ R_{A→B} and t2 ∈ R_A then SN (t1 t2). Prove by induction on SN t2 (since t2 ∈ R_A → SN t2) and SN t1? Let's see the reducts of t1 t2:
- t1 --> t1': need t1' ∈ R_{A→B} (closure under step) — for the SN part, closure under step: SN t1' from SN t1; forall v value ∈ R_A, SN (t1' v) — we know SN (t1 v) and t1 v --> t1' v requires lc v (true for values: v_abs gives lc, v_true/v_false closed); so SN (t1' v) by SN of t1 v. ✓. So closure under step works for R_arrow with this definition (only needs lc v which holds for values).
- t2 --> t2': need SN (t1 t2'): t2' ∈ R_A by closure, and t2' is a reduct, so IH on SN t2. But ST_App2 requires value t1. If t1 is not a value, this case doesn't occur but we can ignore (we only need to prove SN of all actual reducts; proving a stronger statement for all t2' is fine).
- beta: t1 = abs T b, t2 value: reduct open b t2. Need SN (open b t2). We have t1 ∈ R_arrow, so SN (t1 t2) — wait that's what we're proving. Hmm, circular. But we also need SN (open b t2) — where does it come from? From the fundamental theorem at the body, as discussed: for values v ∈ R_A, open b v ∈ R_B (that's what we need to establish for abs to be in R_arrow!). Circular again.

So the definition R_arrow t := SN t ∧ ∀v, V_A v → SN (t v) is insufficient to show abs ∈ R_arrow, because SN (abs v)... wait, SN (abs v) is easy: abs v steps only if v steps (App2) and beta → SN (open b v) — which requires SN (open b v). And SN (open b v) is exactly what we don't have directly; we have lrel B (open b v) from the fundamental theorem, which includes SN. Yes! We do have it: the T_Abs case of the fundamental theorem has hypothesis: for all x fresh, lrel T2 (open t1 x) under env extension. Given v ∈ R_A = lrel T1, we instantiate to get lrel T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v), which includes SN. So SN (open b v) ✓.

Alternatively, avoid needing lc: prove SN/app facts in a way that doesn't rely on ST_App2 applicability. For SN (app (abs T1 b) v): the reducts are those for which a step derivation exists. ST_App2 requires value (abs T1 b), which requires lc. If the term isn't lc, ST_App2 doesn't apply, so there are fewer reducts — fine, we can just prove "for all t', app (abs) v --> t' -> SN t'" and in the ST_App2 case we get value (abs T1 b) as a hypothesis, hence lc available. Hmm OK. But there's still the question of what exactly to include in the arrow relation. Let's design with "lrel B (app t v)" for values v, which is the strongest and most useful, and prove the needed lemmas.

Definition:
```
Fixpoint lrel (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\
       (forall v, value v -> lrel T1 v -> lrel T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
(Using Fixpoint for type-indexed recursion; ty is an inductive with a nested arrow, so Fixpoint on T with recursive calls on T1, T2 is structurally decreasing. ✓)

Properties to prove by induction on T:
1. `lrel_sn : lrel T t -> strongly_normalizing t`. (Bool: identity; Arrow: proj1.)
2. `lrel_step : lrel T t -> t --> t' -> lrel T t'`.
   - Bool: SN closure under step: from SN t and step t t', SN t' (by inversion on SN_intro).
   - Arrow: SN t' from SN t. For all values v ∈ lrel T1: lrel T2 (app t' v) from lrel T2 (app t v) via lrel_step(T2) applied to step (app t v) (app t' v) which uses ST_App1 with lc v. Need lc v: from value v ∈ lrel T1. So need `lrel_lc : lrel T v -> value v -> locally_closed v`? Hmm, values are abs/true/false: need lc of abs T b: value (tm_abs T b) gives locally_closed (tm_abs T b). ✓ Trivially. And for true/false lc holds by lc_true/lc_false. So `value v -> locally_closed v` — prove by inversion on value. Great, doesn't even need lrel.
3. `lrel_expand` or "SN-backward" lemma. Let's think about whether we need it.

The main use: fundamental theorem T_App case: lrel T2 (app t1 t2) from lrel (T1->T2) t1 and lrel T1 t2, where t2 is arbitrary (not necessarily a value). The arrow def only covers values. So we need a lemma:

**Lemma lrel_app : lrel (T1 -> T2) t -> lrel T1 u -> lrel T2 (app t u).**

Prove it by induction on... hmm. Let's attempt by induction on the SN derivation of u (Acc). Actually we can do well-founded induction on `sn u` (Acc step u), with t universally quantified:

```
Lemma lrel_app : forall T1 T2 t u, lrel (T1->T2) t -> lrel T1 u -> lrel T2 (app t u).
```
We need a nested induction. Within it we need to prove lrel T2 (app t u) which is sn + (arrow conditions). If we prove the more general "expand" lemma:

**Lemma lrel_intro_sn : forall T t, strongly_normalizing t -> (forall t', step t t' -> lrel T t') -> lrel T t.**

Wait, is this true for arrow? Given SN t and all reducts in lrel Arrow, show lrel Arrow t: SN t ✓, and forall values v ∈ lrel T1, lrel T2 (app t v). Prove by induction on SN t (Acc), we need lrel T2 (app t v); by lrel_intro_sn at T2 (mutual recursion on types?) we'd need SN (app t v) and all reducts of app t v ∈ lrel T2:
  - SN (app t v): all reducts SN. Reducts:
    - app t' v: lrel T2 by arrow hypothesis at v and lrel_step (lrel Arrow t → t').
    - app t v' (v --> v'): need lrel T2 (app t v'). v' ∈ lrel T1 by lrel_step. By induction on sn v (nested) — hmm, v is SN.
    - open b v: if t = abs T1 b and value v and lc(abs): t has reduct open b v so lrel Arrow (open b v); from that, do we get lrel T2 (open b v)? No! Wait, open b v ∈ lrel Arrow means it's an arrow-type term. But we need lrel T2 (open b v)?? Hmm, that's not right. Let me redo: we're proving lrel T2 (app t v) where t ∈ lrel (T1→T2). The beta reduct open b v should be in lrel T2, since the fundamental theorem says so. But from "all reducts of t are in lrel Arrow" we don't get info about open b v (open b v is a reduct of app t v, not of t). We need the extra hypothesis that t is an abs with open b v ∈ lrel T2 — which we'd get from the arrow lrel hypothesis at v. Indeed, lrel Arrow t with t = abs T1 b gives forall values v ∈ lrel T1, lrel T2 (app t v). But that's what we're proving (app t v ∈ lrel T2)! Circular.

Hmm right: for t = abs T1 b, lrel Arrow t literally says for values v ∈ lrel T1, lrel T2 (app (abs b) v). So in the case t = abs of lrel_intro_sn (where we're proving lrel Arrow t from "all reducts lrel Arrow"), we're stuck: reducts of abs are none, so the hypothesis gives nothing, and we can't derive the arrow property. Unless t = abs and we know open b v ∈ lrel T2 from something else.

So `lrel_intro_sn` (backward closure from SN + reducts) is FALSE for arrow at abs terms. Because the arrow property is not determined by reducts of t (abs has no reducts) but by its body.

Conclusion: the logical relation definition must handle abs differently. Standard fix: define the arrow relation so that it's easy to show for abs, e.g., require values in R_arrow to satisfy the application property only for *values*, AND add a clause: for abs terms, membership follows from the body property. Hmm.

The clean standard approach: define a *value relation* and a *term relation* separately, like:

```
V_Bool v := v = true \/ v = false
V_{A→B} v := exists T b, v = abs T b /\ forall w, V_A w -> R_B (open b w)  -- with types matching
R_T t := SN t /\ forall v, t -->* v -> V_T v    (every value reachable from t is in V_T)
```
Hmm, that's the "evaluation to a value" style relation, common in CBV. Let's explore.

Define `multi` reachability. Or define:
```
R_T t := SN t /\ forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V_T v
```
and V on values:
```
V_Bool v := (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
V_{A→B} v := exists T1 B b, ... = abs T1 b /\ lc /\ forall w, V_A w -> R_B (open b w)
```
This is a common "reducibility" definition for CBV (values of arrow type map related values to related terms). Then:
- Fundamental theorem: need for abs: V_{A→B} (abs T1 b) from forall v ∈ V_A, R_B (open b v) — given by IH at fresh x. But IH gives R_B (subst (open t1 x)) = R_B (open (subst t1) v) — need to relate to open (subst t1) v where open in the value relation is open of the actual body. Should be fine with the right formulation.
- Need: R_T t → SN t ✓.
- Need: R_T closed under step: t → t' then R_T t'. SN ✓; for all t' -->* v, t -->* v, so V ✓.
- Need: R_T (app t u) from R_arrow t and R_A u. This requires: SN of app t u, and all value-reducts of app t u are in V_B. Since t ∈ R_arrow means every value reachable from t is in V_arrow, i.e., of the form abs T1 b with forall w ∈ V_A, R_B (open b w). Then app t u: t reduces to abs, and u reduces to w (value in V_A), then beta gives open b w ∈ R_B, hence SN. And any value reachable from app t u: must be through these chains... We'd need a lemma about multi-step decomposition: if app t u -->* v then either... This is doable but somewhat involved (needs determinism-ish or decomposition of multi steps).

Alternatively the standard "Tait" style with the arrow defined as I had, plus handling abs via a separate lemma. Let me look at how existing Coq developments do CBV SN.

Alternatively: I recall a neat approach for SN of STLC CBV in Coq: define reducibility as:
```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_bool : forall t, SN t -> R Bool t
| R_arrow : forall T1 T2 t, SN t -> (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t v)) -> R (T1 -> T2) t.
```
The abs case: R (T1->T2) (abs T1 b) requires SN (abs) ✓ and forall v value ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs) v). To prove this, do induction on T2? Let's check: we know Hb : forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (open b v) (from fundamental theorem at body). Want lrel T2 (app (abs T1 b) v).

Case T2 = Bool: R Bool (app (abs) v) = SN (app (abs) v). Prove by induction on SN v. Reducts:
  - open b v: R Bool (open b v) = SN ✓ (from Hb).
  - app (abs) v' for v' --> v' (requires value (abs T1 b), true if lc; if not, no such rule, so vacuous; but for the case to arise we get value (abs T1 b) as hypothesis, so it's fine either way — we can just say: in this case, v' ∈ R T1 by closure, hb at v' gives SN (open b v'), and IH on Sn v applies). ✓
  So SN (app (abs) v). ✓

Case T2 = Arrow T3 T4: Need R (T3->T4) (app (abs) v) = SN (app (abs) v) ∧ forall w value ∈ R T3, R T4 (app (app (abs) v) w).
  - SN (app (abs) v): reducts:
      * open b v: SN since Hb v ∈ R (T3->T4) → SN. ✓
      * app (abs) v': need SN (app (abs) v') — by IH on SN v with T2 = Arrow? Wait IH structure.
      * no ST_App1 (abs doesn't step).
    So prove by induction on SN v (Acc): for each reduct need SN; using IH at v'.
  - forall w ∈ R T3: R T4 (app (app (abs) v) w). Hmm: we need to show the term app (app (abs T1 b) v) w is in R T4. We know Hb v : R (T3->T4) (open b v), so for w ∈ R T3 (value), R T4 (app (open b v) w). And app (app (abs) v) w --> app (open b v) w via ST_App1 (needs lc w ✓ value). Then... but we need R T4 (app (app (abs) v) w), i.e., the bigger term, which is a "backward expansion" of app (open b v) w. Hmm. Hmm, this "backward" direction is the tricky part with CBV: the term app (app (abs b) v) w is SN if app (open b v) w is SN and the intermediate terms are SN... **Alternative approach to avoid the substitution machinery**: prove the fundamental theorem directly on the given typing relation using the context Gamma and no explicit substitution! Instead of a substitution interpretation, use the locally nameless "open with fresh fvar" style directly:

State: `Gamma |- t : T` and `sigma` interprets Gamma → R_T t[sigma]? We need some closing. Alternatively, we could use a context interpretation that maps each fvar to a *value* and prove the theorem for terms which are locally closed. Hmm, we still need substitution sigma.

Alternative: Avoid substitution entirely by using the following trick: prove the fundamental theorem in the form

`forall Gamma t T, Gamma |- t : T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R_T (sigma x)) -> R_T (subst sigma t)`

We need subst anyway.

Hmm, could we instead interpret the context via a *single* "closing" function using a parallel substitution that is straightforward to define? Yes, subst is straightforward:

```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```
This is the standard locally-nameless "subst" for fvars (no capture issues since atoms are unique; but bvars in sigma values can be captured under binders — as discussed; we handle with lc).

Then the T_Abs case needs the lemma above. Fine.

Actually, maybe we can avoid the substitution lemma entirely with a different trick: choose the environment sigma to be defined on *all* atoms and use the fact that the fresh x's image is exactly w... The lemma `subst sigma' (open t1 x) = open (subst sigma t1) w` is needed. There's no way around some lemma like this in locally nameless.

Alternatively, use a *different* interpretation of the context: instead of substituting terms for fvars, we can define the logical relation for open terms with a "bvar environment"? No, the context is over fvars.

Let me look at this from the standard literature angle. The standard "reducibility candidates" for CBV (e.g., in "Type Systems" by Cardelli? or the theory of CBV normalization):

A standard approach: define the set of *reducible terms* R_T as follows:

**Alternative standard approach**: Define the logical relation using *evaluation to values* (the "hereditarily" style):

Let `SN` be as given. Define:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t

Hmm, let me look at this from a different angle: what if I use a *value-based* relation with the "terms that are SN and whose reducts stay in R" style, i.e., define R as the *greatest* fixed point? In Coq, inductive definitions give least fixed points; the natural definition of reducibility for SN is inductive (terms reduce to values...). 

Actually — here's a classical clean approach for CBV SN due to Tait/Girard adapted: Use **"hereditarily strongly normalizing"**:
- HSN_Bool t := SN t.
- HSN_{A→B} t := SN t ∧ ∀u, HSN_A u → HSN_B (t u).
This is the CBN-style definition, but for CBV the issue is that t u might get stuck if u is a value but t is not applied... Hmm, actually in CBN the definition works with a different fundamental theorem (no beta issue since SN is closed under substitution etc.). For CBV, `t u` with t ∈ SN and u ∈ HSN: is t u SN? Reducts: t' u, t u', (open b u). The beta case needs open b u ∈ HSN_B. With the fundamental theorem's abs case: abs b ∈ HSN_{A→B} requires ∀u ∈ HSN_A, HSN_B (app (abs) u) — which needs HSN_B (open b u) — from the body typing IH at argument u: HSN_B (open b u) ✓ (if the env interpretation handles it). R_Arrow t := SN t ∧ ∀v, value v → R T1 v → R T2 (app t v)

**Lemma R_app**: forall T1 T2 (t u : tm), R (T1 → T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u).

We need a backward closure lemma. Let's try to prove:

**Lemma R_anti_step (backward)**: forall T (t t' : tm), step t t' -> R T t' -> (forall t'', step t t'' -> t'' = t' ∨ R T t'') -> R T t?
Since our step is deterministic, "forall t'', step t t'' -> R T t''" plus SN? Hmm. If all reducts of t are R T, is t ∈ R T? For Bool (SN): yes, need SN t, which follows from all reducts SN ✓.

So backward closure fails for abs terms (as established). But note: abs terms ARE values, and for value arguments we already have HRt's second component giving R T2 (app t v) directly.   Wait, but the induction hypothesis at T4 is "forall t, ~value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R T4 t') -> R T4 t".   `R_intro_nonvalue : forall t, ~ value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') -> R T t`
  `R_app : forall T1 T2 t u, R (T1→T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)` — where the hard case is non-value u.

Let's see how R_app goes by induction on T2 (with the help of R_intro_nonvalue at T2):

  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') ->

      + ST_AppAbs T' b w: app t v = app (abs T' b) w → t = abs T' b, contradicting ~ value t ✓.
    Hence R T4 (app t v) ✓ by IH_T4.

  Note we used: for t' a reduct of t, H t' Hst : R (T3→T4) t' — gives R T4 (app t' v) when value v, R T3 v ✓. And R_sn at Arrow for SN.

Lemma R_app : forall T1 T2 (t u : tm), R (T1 --> T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u).

```
Definition env := atom -> tm.

Definition env_rel (sigma : atom -> tm) (Gamma : context) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).
```

The arrow component of R (T1→T2) IS only about values: `forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t v)`. So in the fundamental theorem abs case, we only need Hb for *values* v. But R_app_abs as I formulated needs Hb for all R T1 terms. Can we prove a variant that only needs Hb for values? Let's look at R_app_abs's proof again with Hb only for values:

Goal: R T2 (app (abs T1 b) v) for v ∈ R T1 (possibly non-value). Proof: R_intro_nonvalue at T2 with reducts:
- ST_AppAbs: requires value v (from the rule!) ✓ so Hb v (value) Hv ✓.
- ST_App2: app (abs) v' with v --> v'; inner IH on SN v with R T1 v' ✓ (reducts of v).
- ST_App1: vacuous.
So YES — in R_intro_nonvalue, the ST_AppAbs case gives us `value v` as a hypothesis from the step derivation, so Hb only needs values. ✓ 

Let me re-examine: in R_intro_nonvalue at type T2 applied to term app (abs T1 b) v, the reducts: for each r with step (app (abs T1 b) v) r, need R T2 r. Cases by inversion:
- ST_AppAbs T' b' w Hlc Hval: app (abs T1 b) v = app (abs T' b') w → T'=T1, b'=b, w=v; r = open b v. Hb v Hval Hv ✓ (Hval : value v).
- ST_App1: function part tm_abs T1 b steps — impossible ✓.
- ST_App2 v1 t2 t2' Hval Hst: v1 = abs T1 b, t2 = v, t2' with v --> t2'; r = app (abs T1 b) t2'. Inner induction on SN v: R T1 t2' (R_step Hv Hst), then IH t2' Hst' ... hmm, the inner induction hypothesis: forall v', v --> v' -> R T1 v' -> R T2 (app (abs T1 b) v'). ✓ (with fixed b and Hb). 

So R_app_abs with Hb quantified over values only:
```
Lemma R_app_abs : forall T1 T2 b v,
  (forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (open b w)) ->
  R T1 v -> R T2 (app (tm_abs T1 b) v).
```
Proof: induction on SN v (from R_sn Hv), applying R_intro_nonvalue at T2 for the current v. ✓ 

Now the fundamental theorem abs case needs, for each value v with R T1 v, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v). This is derived from IH with sigma' = sigma[x:=v] (needs lc v ← value_lc ✓) and the substitution lemma (needs lc of all sigma images). ✓ 

So R does NOT need lc. 

Now let's double check R_step's arrow case: R_step at Arrow: t --> t', need forall value v ∈ R T1, R T2 (app t' v). Have R T2 (app t v); need app t v --> app t' v via ST_App1 with locally_closed v ← value_lc ✓. Then R_step at T2 ✓.

R_sn at Arrow = proj1; at Bool = id.

Now, are there any other places needing lc? The step rules in lemmas are applied with hypotheses given by the rules themselves ✓. In R_step we supply lc v (value_lc ✓). In the substitution lemma we need lc. In `subst` proofs we need lc notions.

Wait, there's one more thing: does the fundamental theorem need the term t to be locally closed? I don't think so—the env handles free atoms, and R is defined for arbitrary terms (R T t for any t; e.g., R Bool t = SN t, which can hold for non-lc t). Hmm, but is the fundamental theorem true for non-lc terms? A term with free bvars could be typed? Typing is over fvars only, so has_type is only about terms whose bvars... hmm, has_type doesn't mention bvars at all, so a term like `tm_bvar 0` could never be typed (no rule), but a term like `tm_abs T (tm_bvar 1)`? T_Abs requires for all x, `open (tm_abs? ...)` hmm, `open t1 x` where t1 = tm_bvar 1: open_rec 0 x (bvar 1) = bvar 1, so the hypothesis would be `x|->T1;Gamma |- bvar 1 : T2` which is unprovable. So typed terms are morally locally closed. But our theorem doesn't require proving that; the env interpretation gives R of the substituted term. Fine.

Now let's check the T_Abs case more carefully with the actual T_Abs statement:

```
T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1,
  (forall x, ~ In x L -> <{ x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- $(open t1 (tm_fvar x)) \in T2 }>) ->
  <{ Gamma |-- lambda : T1, $(t1) \in T1 -> T2 }>
```
Given: Gamma |- tm_abs T1 t1 : T1 -> T2 (note the conclusion is a specific type T1→T2; in the fundamental theorem with arbitrary T, we invert the typing derivation, so T = T1→T2 ✓).
Need: R (T1→T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)) = R (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
- proj1: SN (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): apply SN_intro; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep — no step rule for tm_abs ✓.
- proj2: forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) v). Use R_app_abs with Hb: forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) w).
  For each such w: choose x fresh (not in L, not in fvars t1). By T_Abs hypothesis: (x|->T1; Gamma) |- open t1 (fvar x) : T2. Apply IH_fundamental with sigma' = update-env sigma x w and Gamma' = update Gamma x T1:
    - env_rel sigma' Gamma': lc images: sigma' y = if x =? y then w else sigma y; lc w ✓ (value w), lc (sigma y) ✓ from env_rel. Typing: if Gamma' y = Some T then (if x =? y then Some T1 else Gamma y) = Some T; if y = x, T = T1 and sigma' y = w with R T1 w ✓; else Gamma y = Some T, sigma' y = sigma y, R T (sigma y) ✓.
    - Conclusion: R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
    - Substitution lemma: subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) w, given x ∉ fvars t1 and lc of all sigma images. ✓

Great.

Now the final theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> SN t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  assert (H : R T (subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t)).
  { apply fundamental; auto. 
    - intros y; simpl; apply lc_fvar.
    - intros x T' Hx; unfold empty in Hx; discriminate. }
  rewrite subst_id in H.
  apply R_sn in H; exact H.
Qed.
```

Wait: env_rel for the identity: `forall x T, empty x = Some T -> R T (fvar x)` — vacuous ✓.

Now let's think about the substitution lemma in detail, since it's the most technical part.

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom := ...  (* union of fvars of subterms *)
```
Need `atom` decidable equality (nat ✓, use Nat.eqb or `eq_dec`).

Freshness: `exists x, ~ In x (L ++ fvars t1)`. Since atoms are nat, use `max_list` or simpler: pick `x = S (fold_right max 0 (L ++ fvars t1))`. Or use a standard lemma `not_in_cons`... Let me write:

```
Lemma fresh_atom : forall (l : list atom), exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (fold_right max 0 l)). 
  (* prove not in l by induction: all elements ≤ fold max *)
```
Need lemma: forall l y, In y l -> y <= fold_right max 0 l. Then S (max) > y. Since `In y l -> y ≤ fold max 0 l < S (fold max 0 l)`. Use `Nat.lt_succ_r` / `PeanoNat.Nat.le_lt_trans`. Or simpler: use `max_list` from Stdlib? There's `List.max_list` in some versions. Let's just do it manually.

Alternatively, a slicker approach: since atoms are nat, we can use the fact that `forall l, exists x, ~ In x l` by induction on l:
```
Lemma fresh_atom : forall l, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| a l [x Hx]].
  - exists 0; simpl; auto.
  - exists (if Nat.eqb x a then S x else x); intros [H | H]; ...
```
Hmm, need to handle x = a. Let's do: exists (max (S x) (S a))? Let's think: given x ∉ l, we need y ∉ a :: l. Choose y = if x =? a then S x else x. If y = x (x ≠ a): y ∉ l ✓ and y ≠ a ✓. If y = S x = a? impossible since a = x. Fine. Let me write it with Nat.eqb and lemmas about it. Actually simpler: choose y = S (x + a)? Since x ∉ l, y ≠ x so y ∉ l (if y ∈ l then x... no wait, y ≠ x doesn't imply y ∉ l; l could contain y!). Hmm no: we know x ∉ l, but we need y ∉ l. Since we only know one x ∉ l, choosing y = S x could still be in l. Hmm.

Better: induction on l with a "max" approach: use the maximal element + 1.

```
Lemma exists_fresh : forall l, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l.
  induction l as [| a l IH].
  - exists 0; auto.
  - destruct IH as [x Hx].
    exists (Nat.max (S x) (S a)).
    intros [Hin | Hin].
    + apply Nat.max_lub... 
```
Let's check: y = max (S x) (S a). y ≠ a since y ≥ S a > a ✓. y ∉ l since y ≥ S x > x and x ∉ l? Wait, y > x and x ∉ l does NOT imply y ∉ l. Ugh, wrong direction: Hx says x ∉ l; y could be in l if y ≠ x. Hmm.

OK so induction on the list doesn't directly work with "one witness". Use maximum: y = 1 + max of list. Prove by a lemma: `forall l, forall y, In y l -> y < S (fold_right Nat.max 0 l)`. That's straightforward.

Alternatively, since the environment is over nat and we need freshness w.r.t. L ++ fvars t1, another approach: use a lemma about `List.fold_right Nat.max`. Let me write:

```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | a :: l => Nat.max a (max_list l)
  end.

Lemma in_max_list : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l; simpl; intros x H.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l); [apply IHl; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh : forall l, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (max_list l)). intros H.
  apply in_max_list in H. apply Nat.lt_irrefl with (S (max_list l)). 
  (* H : S (max_list l) <= max_list l *)
  apply Nat.le_lt_trans with (max_list l)... 
```
`lia` can finish: `assert (S (max_list l) <= max_list l) by auto; lia.` Good.

Now, we also want the fresh x to satisfy multiple conditions: not in L, not in fvars t1. Use l = L ++ fvars t1 and then ~In x (L ++ fvars t1) gives both via `in_app_iff` (List.in_app_iff). ✓

Substitution lemma:
```
Lemma subst_open : forall sigma t x w,
  ~ In x (fvars t) ->
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then w else sigma y) (open t (tm_fvar x))
    = open (subst sigma t) w.
```
Hmm, wait: I previously derived that we need lc of sigma images for the fvar case: LHS = sigma' y = sigma y for y ≠ x; RHS = open_rec 0 w (sigma y) — needs sigma y to have no free bvars. But careful: open_rec 0 w (sigma y) = sigma y requires sigma y to be locally_closed *at level 0*, i.e., lc_at 0. ✓ 

But also the generalized version for arbitrary k:
```
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma t x w k,
  ~ In x (fvars t) ->
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then w else sigma y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
    = open_rec k w (subst sigma t).
```
Proof by induction on t; generalizing k.
- tm_bvar i: simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb k i); refl. Note: `open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if Nat.eqb k i then fvar x else bvar i`; subst of fvar x = sigma' x = w (need Nat.eqb x x = true ✓). RHS: open_rec k w (subst sigma (bvar i)) = open_rec k w (bvar i) = if k =? i then w else bvar i. Need the eqbs to match: `Nat.eqb k i` vs `Nat.eqb k i` ✓ same.
  Wait: LHS = subst sigma' (if k =? i then fvar x else bvar i) = if k =? i then subst sigma' (fvar x) else bvar i = if k =? i then w else bvar i. Need sigma' x = w: sigma' x = if x =? x then w else sigma x = w ✓ (Nat.eqb_refl).
- tm_fvar y: simpl. LHS = sigma' y. RHS = open_rec k w (sigma y). 
  * If y = x: then x ∈ fvars t → contradiction with ~In x (fvars t) (need fvars lemma: fvars (fvar x) = [x]; simpl should give it). So y ≠ x. Then sigma' y = sigma y (Nat.eqb x y = false — need to convert y ≠ x to eqb x y = false; use `Nat.eqb_neq` or `apply Nat.eqb_neq; auto`). And RHS = open_rec k w (sigma y) = sigma y since sigma y is locally_closed. Need lemma: `lc_open_rec : locally_closed t -> forall k u, open_rec k u t = t`. Prove by induction on t? Hmm, for locally_closed t: bvar i occurs at depth d with i < d, so eqb (k+d) i = false. Let me prove:
    ```
    Lemma lc_at_open_rec : forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall j u, j >= k -> open_rec j u t = t.
    ```
    Hmm, let's think: for lc_at k t, bvars at depth d satisfy i < k + d. open_rec j replaces bvar (j + d) at depth d (since open_rec increments by depth). We need j + d ≠ i; since i < k + d ≤ j + d ✓. So statement: forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall j u, k <= j -> open_rec j u t = t.
    Proof by induction on t (generalizing k j):
    - bvar i: lc_bvar gives i < k ≤ j. So eqb j i = false. Need lemma: i < j → Nat.eqb j i = false. By `Nat.eqb_eq`? `apply Nat.eqb_neq; lia` hmm `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m`. So `rewrite Nat.eqb_neq; lia`. ✓
    - fvar, true, false: simpl; refl.
    - app: simpl; rewrite IH1, IH2 with same k j.
    - abs T t1: lc_abs with lc_at (S k) t1. Goal: abs T (open_rec (S j) u t1) = abs T t1. Apply IH1 with (S k) (S j): need S k ≤ S j ✓ by lia. ✓
  * So the fvar case works.
- app: simpl; congruence with IHs. Need ~ In x (fvars t1) and ~ In x (fvars t2) from ~ In x (fvars (app t1 t2)) — with fvars (app t1 t2) = fvars t1 ++ fvars t2, use `in_app_iff`. ✓

- true/false ✓.

So I need fvars defined such that simpl gives the right shapes:
```
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2

Now, the env_rel with lc images: In the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case, when we extend the env with w (a value), lc w ✓. Also the initial env (identity) has lc images ✓ (fvar is lc).

Hmm wait, actually we also need to double check: does the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case need lc of the environment images? Yes, for subst_open we need `forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)`. We have that from env_rel.

Now, there's one more consideration: Is the fundamental theorem statement strong enough for the T_Abs case, where we apply the IH to `open t1 (fvar x)` — the IH is the theorem itself (proven by induction on the typing derivation). We need to structure the proof by induction on the has_type derivation. Since the theorem statement quantifies over Gamma, t, T, and then sigma, we do `intros Gamma t T Hty; induction Hty; intros sigma Henv`. The T_Abs case's IH: `forall sigma, env_rel sigma (update x T1 Gamma)?` Hmm, let's see. The induction principle for has_type:

```
has_type_ind :
 forall P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop,
 (forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> P Gamma (fvar x) T) ->
 (forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1,
    (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->
    P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 --> T2)) ->
 ...
```
So the IH in the T_Abs case is: `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` where P Gamma' t' T' := forall sigma, env_rel sigma Gamma' -> R T' (subst sigma t'). 

So the theorem statement should be `forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, env_rel sigma Gamma -> R T (subst sigma t)`. And P is exactly that after intros. ✓

Now, one concern: in the T_Abs case, the conclusion needs R (T1→T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)). `subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)` by simpl ✓.

Let me also consider whether the fundamental theorem needs the typing hypothesis's side conditions: T_Abs's L is a list; we choose x fresh from L ++ fvars t1. ✓

Now let's also verify the App case of the fundamental theorem: 
IH1 : forall sigma, env_rel sigma Gamma -> R (T1 --> T2) (subst sigma t1).
IH2 : forall sigma, env_rel sigma Gamma -> R T1 (subst sigma t2).
Goal: R T2 (subst sigma (tm_app t1 t2)) = R T2 (tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)).
Apply R_app with H1 := IH1 sigma Henv and H2 := IH2 sigma Henv. ✓

T_Var: Goal R T (subst sigma (tm_fvar x)) = R T (sigma x). From Henv: Gamma x = Some T → R T (sigma x) ✓.

T_True: R Bool (subst sigma tm_true) = R Bool tm_true = SN tm_true. Prove SN true: apply SN_intro; intros t' H; inversion H ✓ (no step rule for tm_true).
T_False similarly.

Now the R definitions and lemmas. Let me carefully write them.

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
This is a Fixpoint with a decreasing argument T; recursive calls on T1 and T2 which are subterms ✓. Note: Coq might complain about the recursive call `R T1 v` inside the arrow branch — T1 is a subterm of Ty_Arrow T1 T2 ✓, structurally decreasing ✓.

Now lemmas:

```
Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof. intros T; induction T; simpl; intros t H; [exact H | exact (proj1 H)]. Qed.
```
Hmm, careful: `induction T` then `simpl` — R T t should reduce. Actually I can do `destruct T; simpl; ...`. Since R is a Fixpoint on T, `destruct T; simpl` should unfold ✓.

```
Lemma value_lc : forall v, value v -> locally_closed v.
Proof. intros v Hv; induction Hv; simpl; auto using lc_at. ... Qed.
```
Hmm, `value` constructors: v_abs T t Hlc → locally_closed (tm_abs T t) = Hlc ✓; v_true → lc_true 0; v_false → lc_false 0. `induction Hv` or `destruct`. Let me write `intros v Hv; destruct Hv; simpl; auto using lc_true, lc_false.` Hmm, `lc_true : forall k, lc_at k tm_true`, so `lc_true 0` — `auto` with `lc_at` constructors might work: `constructor`. Let me write explicit: `destruct Hv; simpl; [exact H | apply lc_true | apply lc_false]`. Hmm, but `locally_closed` is a Definition so `simpl` unfolds. Actually the goal after destruct is `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` with hypothesis `H : locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` — exact H ✓.

```
Lemma app_not_value : forall t1 t2, ~ value (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof. intros t1 t2 H; inversion H. Qed.
```
Does inversion work? value has constructors whose conclusions are tm_abs, tm_true, tm_false; inversion on `value (tm_app t1 t2)` should find no matching constructor and close the goal ✓.

```
Lemma sn_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t t' Hsn Hst; inversion Hsn; auto. Qed.
```
Hmm, inversion on `SN t` where t is a variable: `inversion Hsn` gives the constructor with `forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'` — since SN_intro is the only constructor, inversion gives a hypothesis `H : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'` (roughly) and then `apply H; auto`. But inversion on an inductive whose constructor has a premise with the same inductive (recursive) may generate the recursive hypothesis too. Let's use `destruct Hsn` hmm — SN has one constructor with one argument: `destruct Hsn as [t' Hred]`? The constructor argument is `forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'`, and t is an index. `destruct Hsn eqn:` might substitute t with... Since SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', ...) -> SN t, destruct Hsn gives the premise. Let's just write `intros t t' Hsn Hst; destruct Hsn as [Hred]; apply Hred; assumption.` Hmm, careful: `destruct Hsn as [Hred]` — the pattern for a constructor with one argument. But there may be issues with the index t. Alternatively `inversion Hsn as [Hred]; auto.` I'll test in Coq.

Now R_step:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T; induction T; intros t t' HR Hst.
  - simpl in *; apply sn_step with t; assumption.
  - simpl in *. destruct HR as [Hsn Harr]. split.
    + apply sn_step with t; assumption.
    + intros v Hval HRv. apply (IHT2 (tm_app t v) (tm_app t' v)); [apply Harr; assumption | apply ST_App1; assumption || apply value_lc; assumption].
```
Hmm, ST_App1 requires `t --> t'` and `locally_closed t2`. Wait, ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2. In my naming: step t t' gives app t v --> app t' v with lc v ✓.

Careful with the induction on T: `induction T` gives IHT1 and IHT2 in the arrow case ✓ (both subterms). But note the order: T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2, and R T2 is used → IHT2 ✓.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: `simpl in HR` unfolds R. Since R is a Fixpoint, `simpl` should unfold for concrete T. But in the induction, after `induction T`, the T1/T2 cases have concrete constructors ✓.

Wait, one problem: in the arrow case, the goal is R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t' and after simpl it's `SN t' /\ forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t' v)`. ✓ IHT2 : forall t t', R T2 t -> step t t' -> R T2 t'. ✓

R_intro_nonvalue:
```
Lemma R_intro_nonvalue : forall T t,
  ~ value t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') ->
  R T t.
Proof.
  intros T; induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; intros t Hnv Hred.
  - simpl. apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst. apply Hred in Hst. simpl in Hst. exact Hst.
  - simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst. apply Hred in Hst. simpl in Hst. exact (proj1 Hst).
    + intros v Hval HRv. apply IH2. 
      * apply app_not_value.
      * intros r Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
        -- (* ST_AppAbs *) 
           (* app t v = app (abs T0 b) w *) 
           ... need to handle that t = abs ... contradicting Hnv.
        -- (* ST_App1 *) apply Hred in Hst...
        -- (* ST_App2 *) contradiction with Hnv
```
Hmm, the inversion cases: for ST_AppAbs, `inversion Hstep` on a step with conclusion `tm_app t v --> r` where t is a variable: the ST_AppAbs constructor's conclusion is `tm_app (tm_abs T t0) v0 --> open t0 v0`. Unification with `tm_app t v` gives t = tm_abs T t0, which contradicts Hnv : ~ value t (since tm_abs with lc is a value — careful: v_abs requires locally_closed; the step hypothesis gives it ✓). So `subst; exfalso; apply Hnv; apply v_abs; assumption`.

For ST_App2: conclusion `tm_app v1 t2 --> tm_app v1 t2'` unifying with `tm_app t v`: v1 = t, t2 = v; hypothesis `value v1` = `value t` contradicts Hnv ✓.

For ST_App1: conclusion `tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2`, with t1 = t, t2 = v, t1 --> t1'; r = app t1' v. We need R T2 (app t1' v). From Hred t1' Hst : R (T1 --> T2) t1' (after simpl: SN t1' ∧ forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t1' v)). Apply its second component with v, Hval, HRv ✓.

So:

Lemma R_app : forall T1 T2 (t u : tm),
  R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u).

  intros T1 T2 t u Ht Hu.
  assert (Hsn : strongly_normalizing t) by (apply (R_sn (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)); exact Ht).
  revert u Hu Ht.
  induction Hsn as [t Hred IHout].

  assert (Hsnu : strongly_normalizing u) by (apply (R_sn T1); exact Hu).
  revert Hu Ht.

  intros Hu Ht.
  destruct (value_dec u) as [Hv | Hnv].

With Coq 9.1 / Rocq, the scheme for `strongly_normalizing`:

Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  SN_intro : forall t : tm, (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t.

The generated `strongly_normalizing_ind`:
```
forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t : tm,
      (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') ->
      (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> P t') ->
      P t) ->
  forall t : tm, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```
Hmm, does Coq include the original premise (with strongly_normalizing) in the induction principle? For strictly positive inductives, Coq's scheme includes the constructor's arguments, with recursive occurrences replaced by P... Actually no: Coq's default scheme replaces the *whole* recursive premise? Let me recall: For `nat`, `nat_ind : forall P, P 0 -> (forall n, P n -> P (S n)) -> forall n, P n` — the recursive occurrence `nat` in the constructor's argument type `nat -> nat`... wait `S : nat -> nat` has argument nat, and the scheme gives P n for the recursive argument. For SN_intro whose argument is `forall t', step t t' -> SN t'`, the recursive occurrence is under a binder; Coq's scheme gives `forall t', step t t' -> P t'`. I believe Coq's default scheme does NOT include the original recursive premise separately for such cases... Hmm, actually for inductives where the recursive occurrence appears in a premise, the scheme replaces it with P and keeps the rest. For example:

```
Inductive foo : nat -> Prop :=
| foo_intro : forall n, (forall m, m < n -> foo m) -> foo n.
```
The generated `foo_ind` is: `forall P, (forall n, (forall m, m < n -> P m) -> P n) -> forall n, foo n -> P n`. I'm fairly confident Coq replaces the recursive occurrences (under binders) with P and does not keep the original. Lemma subst_open_rec : forall (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) (x : atom) (w : tm) (k : nat),

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) {struct t} : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i

```coq
(** Substitution on free variables. *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) {struct t} : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; simpl; congruence.
Qed.
```

For app: `subst id t1 = t1` and `subst id t2 = t2` → f_equal. `induction t; simpl; f_equal; auto` should work? For bvar: `tm_bvar n = tm_bvar n` ✓ refl; fvar: `tm_fvar a = tm_fvar a` ✓; app: `tm_app (subst ...) (subst ...) = tm_app t1 t2` needs congruence with IHs. `induction t; simpl; congruence` might work but let's try `induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; congruence.` Or just `induction t; simpl; f_equal; auto.`

Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | cons a l' => Nat.max a (max_list l')

(** The logical relation. *)
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```

R_step:
```coq
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T; induction T; intros t t' HR Hst; simpl in *.
  - (* Bool *) apply sn_step with t; assumption.
  - (* Arrow *) destruct HR as [Hsn Harr]. split.
    + apply sn_step with t; assumption.
    + intros v Hval HRv.
      apply IHT2 with (t := tm_app t v).
      * apply Harr; assumption.
      * apply ST_App1; [assumption | apply value_lc; assumption].
Qed.
```

Definition env_rel (sigma : atom -> tm) (Gamma : context) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) /\

```coq
(** ** Substitution and free variables *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) {struct t} : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.
```

```coq
(* ---------- Substitution ---------- *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) {struct t} : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fvars t1) (fvars t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; f_equal; assumption.
Qed.

(* ---------- Fresh atoms ---------- *)

Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | cons a l' => Nat.max a (max_list l')
  end.

Lemma in_max_list : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| a l IH]; simpl; intros x H.
  - destruct H.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l); [apply IH; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom : forall l, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (max_list l)). intros H.
  apply in_max_list in H.
  assert (S (max_list l) < S (max_list l)) by lia.
  apply Nat.lt_irrefl in H0. exact H0.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `apply Nat.lt_irrefl in H0` — Nat.lt_irrefl : forall n, ~ n < n; applying to H0 : S m < S m gives False ✓. Actually `exact (Nat.lt_irrefl _ H0)` ✓.

```coq
(* ---------- Local closure and opening ---------- *)

Lemma lc_at_open_rec : forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall j u, k <= j -> open_rec j u t = t.
Proof.
  intros t. induction t; intros k Hlc j u Hkj; simpl.
  - inversion Hlc as [k' i Hi | | | | |]; subst.
    destruct (Nat.eqb j n) eqn:E; [| reflexivity].

  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

```coq
(* ---------- The logical relation ---------- *)

Fixpoint lrel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> lrel T1 v -> lrel T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.

Lemma lrel_sn : forall T t, lrel T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros T; induction T; simpl; intros t H.
  - exact H.
  - exact (proj1 H).
Qed.

Lemma value_lc : forall v, value v -> locally_closed v.
Proof.
  intros v Hv; induction Hv; simpl; auto using lc_true, lc_false.
Qed.

Lemma app_not_value : forall t1 t2, ~ value (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof. intros t1 t2 H; inversion H. Qed.

Lemma sn_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn Hst.
  induction Hsn as [t Hred IH].
  apply Hred; exact Hst.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]` — the scheme has 3 arguments to the constructor pattern? The generated scheme is `forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> ...`. So `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]` ✓ (t, Hred, IH).

But wait: Hsn : SN t where t is a variable in context, and Hst : step t t'. When we do `induction Hsn`, Coq generalizes t but Hst mentions t! So `induction Hsn` would fail because Hst depends on t. Let me revert Hst first? Hmm, the goal is `SN t'` and Hst : step t t'. If I revert Hst, the goal becomes `step t t' -> SN t'` and induction on Hsn : SN t generalizes t... but t' also appears. Hmm.

Simpler: use `inversion Hsn` or `destruct Hsn`:
```
Lemma sn_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn Hst.
  destruct Hsn as [Hred].  (* or inversion *)
  apply (Hred t' Hst).
Qed.
```
Does `destruct Hsn as [Hred]` work? Hsn : SN t with t a variable. destruct on an inductive with index t: Coq will substitute? For a Prop with one constructor whose result is `SN t`, destruct will unify t with the constructor's argument. Hmm, `destruct Hsn` when t is a variable: Coq replaces t by the constructor's implicit argument (fresh variable), giving Hred : forall t', step t t' -> SN t' ✓. I think destruct works. But the index issue: `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. Destructing Hsn : SN t unifies the constructor's result SN ?t with SN t → ?t := t ✓.

Let me test. If destruct is problematic, `inversion Hsn as [Hred]` should work.

Actually, there's a subtlety: since strongly_normalizing is a Prop with a single constructor whose recursive argument is under binders, `destruct` will also generate the IH? No—destruct does not generate induction hypotheses (that's `induction`). destruct just does case analysis ✓ giving Hred.

R_step:
```coq
Lemma lrel_step : forall T t t', lrel T t -> step t t' -> lrel T t'.
Proof.
  intros T; induction T; intros t t' HR Hst; simpl in *.
  - apply sn_step with t; assumption.
  - destruct HR as [Hsn Harr]. split.
    + apply sn_step with t; assumption.
    + intros v Hval HRv.
      apply IHT2 with (t := tm_app t v).
      * apply Harr; assumption.
      * apply ST_App1; [assumption | apply value_lc; assumption].
Qed.
```
Hmm: `apply IHT2 with (t := tm_app t v)` — IHT2 : forall t t', lrel T2 t -> step t t' -> lrel T2 t'. So `apply IHT2` with t := app t v and t' := app t' v; the goal lrel T2 (app t' v) matches the conclusion with t := app t v ✓. Then subgoals: lrel T2 (app t v) and step (app t v) (app t' v). ✓

But careful: `simpl in *` also simplifies HR and Hst; Hst : step t t' stays.

Now lrel_intro_nonvalue:

```coq
Lemma lrel_intro_nonvalue : forall T t,
  ~ value t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> lrel T t') ->
  lrel T t.

Lemma lrel_app : forall T1 T2 (t u : tm),
  lrel (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t -> lrel T1 u -> lrel T2 (tm_app t u).
Proof.

  revert Ht Hu u.

```coq
Definition env_rel (sigma : atom -> tm) (Gamma : context) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) /\
  (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> lrel T (sigma x)).

Lemma env_rel_update : forall (sigma : atom -> tm) Gamma x T w,
  env_rel sigma Gamma ->
  value w ->
  lrel T w ->
  env_rel (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then w else sigma y) (update Gamma x T).
Proof.
  intros sigma Gamma x T w [Hlc Hty] Hval Hw. split.
  - intros y. simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply value_lc; assumption.
    + apply Hlc.
  - intros y T' Hy. simpl in Hy. simpl.
    destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. injection Hy as Heq. subst T'. 
      (* goal lrel T w *) exact Hw.
    + apply Hty. exact Hy.
Qed.
```

Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  assert (H : lrel T (subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t)).

Qed.

```

  step (tm_app t u) r ->
